<p><b>Ramon Llull</b><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_Saint_Francis" title="Third Order of Saint Francis">TOSF</a></span> (<small>Catalan:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ca-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Catalan" title="Help:IPA/Catalan">[rəˈmoɲ ˈʎuʎ]</a></span>; c. 1232<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> –  1315/1316) was a <a href="/wiki/Philosopher" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosopher">philosopher</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Theologian">theologian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poet" title="Poet">poet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">missionary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christian_apologist" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian apologist">Christian apologist</a> and former knight from the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Majorca" title="Kingdom of Majorca">Kingdom of Majorca</a>.
</p><p>He invented a philosophical system known as the <i>Art</i>, conceived as a type of universal logic to prove the truth of Christian doctrine to interlocutors of all faiths and nationalities. The <i>Art</i> consists of a set of general principles and combinatorial operations. It is illustrated with diagrams.
</p><p>A prolific writer, he is also known for his literary works written in <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a>, which he composed to make his <i>Art</i> accessible to a wider audience. In addition to <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> he also probably wrote in <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a> (although no texts in Arabic survive). His books were translated into <a href="/wiki/Occitan_language" title="Occitan language">Occitan</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Castilian</a> during his lifetime.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although his work did not enjoy huge success during his lifetime, he has had a rich and continuing reception. In the early modern period his name became associated with <a href="/wiki/Alchemy" title="Alchemy">alchemical works</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> More recently he has been recognized as a precursor of the modern liberal voting franchise 450 years before <a href="/wiki/Jean-Charles_de_Borda" title="Jean-Charles de Borda">Borda</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marquis_de_Condorcet" title="Marquis de Condorcet">Condorcet</a> had proposed the idea and also the computer and a pioneer of <a href="/wiki/Computation_theory" class="mw-redirect" title="Computation theory">computation theory</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Philosophy,_Vol_1958_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Philosophy,_Vol_1958-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBonner2007290_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner2007290-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-knuth_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-knuth-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_family">Early life and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life and family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG/220px-LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="211" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG/330px-LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG/440px-LifeOfRaymondLull14thCentury.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2057" data-file-height="1969" /></a><figcaption>Scenes from the life of Raymond Lull, in a 14th-century manuscript</figcaption></figure>
<p>Llull was born in <a href="/wiki/Palma_de_Mallorca" title="Palma de Mallorca"> Palma</a> into a wealthy family of <a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a> patricians who had come to the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Majorca" title="Kingdom of Majorca">Kingdom of Majorca</a> (actually <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>) in 1229 with the conquering armies of <a href="/wiki/James_I_of_Aragon" title="James I of Aragon">James I of Aragon</a>. James I had conquered the formerly <a href="/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate" title="Almohad Caliphate">Almohad</a>-ruled Majorca as part of a larger move to integrate the territories of the  <a href="/wiki/Balearic_Islands" title="Balearic Islands">Balearic Islands</a> (now part of <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>) into the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a>. Llull was born there a few years later, in 1232 or 1233. Muslims still constituted a large part of the population of Majorca and Jews were present in cultural and economic affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBonner20071_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner20071-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1257 Llull married <a href="/w/index.php?title=Blanca_Picany&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Blanca Picany (page does not exist)">Blanca Picany</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://ca.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blanca_Picany" class="extiw" title="ca:Blanca Picany">ca</a>&#93;</span>, with whom he had two children, Domingo and Magdalena.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Although he formed a family, he lived what he would later call the licentious and worldly life of a <a href="/wiki/Troubadour" title="Troubadour">troubadour</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_calling">Religious calling</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Religious calling">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1263 Llull experienced a series of <a href="/wiki/Vision_(spirituality)" title="Vision (spirituality)">visions</a>. He narrates the event in his autobiography <i>Vita coaetanea</i> ("A Contemporary Life"):
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Ramon, while still a young man and Seneschal to the King of Majorca, was very given to composing worthless songs and poems and to doing other licentious things. One night he was sitting beside his bed, about to compose and write in his vulgar tongue a song to a lady whom he loved with a foolish love; and as he began to write this song, he looked to his right and saw our Lord <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> <a href="/wiki/Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ">Christ</a> on the Cross, as if suspended in mid-air.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The vision came to Llull five times in all and inspired in him three intentions: to give up his soul for the sake of God’s love and honor, to convert the <a href="/wiki/Saracen" title="Saracen">Saracens</a> (i.e., Arabs and/or Muslims) to Christianity, and write the best book in the world against the errors of the unbelievers.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following his visions he sold his possessions on the model of Saint  <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a> and set out on pilgrimages to the shrines of Saint Mary of <a href="/wiki/Rocamadour" title="Rocamadour">Rocamadour</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_the_Great" title="James the Great">Saint James</a>, and other places, never to come back to his family and profession. When he returned to Majorca he purchased a Muslim slave in order to learn Arabic from him.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> For the next nine years, until 1274, he engaged in study and contemplation in relative solitude. He read extensively in both Latin and Arabic, learning both Christian and Muslim theological and philosophical thought.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1270 Llull founded the hermitage of the Holy Trinity in Mallorca, known as Miramar. <sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1271 and 1274 Llull wrote his first works, a compendium of the Muslim thinker <a href="/wiki/Al-Ghazali" title="Al-Ghazali">Al-Ghazali</a>'s logic and the <i>Llibre de contemplació en Déu</i> (<i>Book on the Contemplation of God</i>), a lengthy guide to finding truth through contemplation.
</p><p>In 1274, while staying at a hermitage on <a href="/wiki/Puig_de_Randa" title="Puig de Randa">Puig de Randa</a>, the form of the great book Llull was to write was finally given to him through divine revelation: a complex system that he named his <i>Art</i>, which would become the motivation behind most of his life's efforts.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Missionary_work_and_education">Missionary work and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Missionary work and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Llull urged the study of Arabic and other then-insufficiently studied languages in Europe,<sup id="cite_ref-Johnston1996_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Johnston1996-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> along with most of his works, to convert Muslims and schismatic Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-Blum2013_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blum2013-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He travelled through Europe to meet with <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">popes</a>, kings, and princes, trying to establish special colleges to prepare future missionaries.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 1276 a language school for Franciscan missionaries was founded at Miramar, funded by the King of Majorca.<sup id="cite_ref-univbarc_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-univbarc-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>About 1291 he went to Tunis, preached to the Saracens, disputed with them in philosophy, and after another brief sojourn in Paris, returned to the East as a missionary.<sup id="cite_ref-Turner_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Turner-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Llull travelled to Tunis a second time in about 1304, and wrote numerous letters to the king of Tunis, but little else is known about this part of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-Classen2018_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Classen2018-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>He returned in 1308, reporting that the conversion of Muslims should be achieved through prayer, not through military force. He finally achieved his goal of linguistic education at major universities in 1311 when the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Vienne" title="Council of Vienne">Council of Vienne</a> ordered the creation of chairs of <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a> and Chaldean (<a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a>) at the universities of <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>, Paris, and <a href="/wiki/Salamanca" title="Salamanca">Salamanca</a> as well as at the Papal Court.<sup id="cite_ref-Classen2018_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Classen2018-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Llull called for the expulsion of Jews who were unwilling to convert to Christianity, and influenced later European monarchs to expel Jews in practice.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Llull4.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Llull4.jpg/220px-Llull4.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Llull4.jpg/330px-Llull4.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Llull4.jpg/440px-Llull4.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption>Llull's tomb in Palma</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1314, at the age of 82, Llull traveled again to Tunis, possibly prompted by the correspondence between <a href="/wiki/James_II_of_Aragon" title="James II of Aragon">King James II of Aragon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abd_al-Wahid_Zakariya_ibn_al-Lihyani" title="Abd al-Wahid Zakariya ibn al-Lihyani">al-Lihyani</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Hafsid" class="mw-redirect" title="Hafsid">Hafsid</a> <a href="/wiki/Caliphate" title="Caliphate">caliph</a>, indicating that the caliph wished to convert to Christianity. Whereas Llull had been met with difficulties during his previous visits to North Africa, he was allowed to operate this time without interference from the authorities due to the improved relations between Tunis and Aragon.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His last work is dated December 1315 in Tunis. The circumstances of his death remain unknown. He probably died sometime between then and March 1316, either in Tunis, on the ship on the return voyage, or in Majorca upon his return.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Llull's tomb, created in 1448, is in the Franciscan church in Palma, Majorca.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Llull.E2.80.99s_Art"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Llull’s_Art">Llull’s <i>Art</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Llull’s Art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Llull’s <i>Art</i> (in Latin <i>Ars</i>) is at the center of his thought and undergirds his entire corpus. It is a system of universal logic based on a set of general principles activated in a combinatorial process. It can be used to prove statements about God and Creation (e.g., God is a Trinity). Often the <i>Art</i> formulates these statements as questions and answers (e.g., Q: Is there a Trinity in God? A: Yes.). It works cumulatively through an iterative process; statements about God's nature must be proved for each of His essential attributes in order to prove the statement true for God (i.e., Goodness is threefold, Greatness is threefold, Eternity is threefold, Power is threefold, etc.).
</p><p>What sets Llull's system apart is its unusual use of letters and diagrams, giving it an algebraic or algorithmic character. He developed the <i>Art</i> over the course of many decades, writing new books to explain each new version. The <i>Art</i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>s trajectory can be divided into two main phases, although each phase contains numerous variations. The first is sometimes called the Quaternary Phase (1274 - 1290) and the second the Ternary Phase (1290 - 1308). This terminology was coined by Anthony Bonner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBonner2007121_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner2007121-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Quaternary_Phase">Quaternary Phase</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Quaternary Phase">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The two main works of the Quaternary Phase are the <i>Ars compendiosa inveniendi veritatem</i> (ca. 1274) and the <i>Ars demonstrativa</i> (ca. 1283).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBonner200726_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner200726-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Ars demonstrativa</i> has twelve main figures. A set of sixteen principles, or 'dignities' (divine attributes) comprise the general foundation for the system's operation. These are contained in the first figure (Figure A) and assigned letters (B through R). The rest of the figures enable the user to take these principles and elaborate to demonstrate the truth of statements. Figure T is important because it contains "relational principles"(i.e. minority, majority, equality), also assigned letters. The <i>Art</i> then lists combinations of letters as a sort of visual aid for the process of working through every possible combination of principles. Figure S displays the Augustinian powers of the soul (will, intellect, and memory) and their acts (willing, understanding, remembering). Figure S was eliminated from the <i>Art</i> after 1290. Even in subsequent versions of the <i>Art</i> Llull maintained that the powers of the soul needed to be in alignment for a proper operation of the <i>Art</i>. This differentiates Llull's system from Aristotelian logic. Because classical logic did not take the powers of the soul into account it was ill-equipped to handle theological issues, in Llull's view.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ternary_Phase">Ternary Phase</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Ternary Phase">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull inaugurated the Ternary Phase with two works written in 1290: the <i>Ars inventiva veritatis</i> and the <i>Art amativa</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBonner2007121_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner2007121-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> The culmination of this phase came in 1308 with a finalized version of the <i>Art</i> called the <i>Ars generalis ultima</i>. In the same year Llull wrote an abbreviated version called the <i>Ars brevis</i>. In these works Llull revised the <i>Art</i> to have only four main figures. He reduced the number of divine principles in the first figure to nine (goodness, greatness, eternity, power, wisdom, will, virtue, truth, glory). Figure T also now has nine relational principles (difference, concordance, contrariety, beginning, middle, end, majority, equality, minority), reduced from fifteen. Llull kept the combinatorial aspect of the process.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Correlatives">Correlatives</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Correlatives">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull introduced an aspect of the system called the "correlatives" just before the final transition to the Ternary Phase. The correlatives first appear in a work called the <i>Lectura super figuras Artis demonstrativae</i> (c.1285-7) and came to undergird his formulation of the <a href="/wiki/Ontology" title="Ontology">nature of being</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The doctrine of correlatives stipulates that everything, at the level of being, has a threefold structure: agent, patient, act. For example, the divine principle "goodness" consists of "that which does good" (agent), "that which receives good" (patient), and "to do good" (act). Llull developed a system of Latin suffixes to express the correlatives, i.e. <i>bonitas</i> (goodness); <i>bonificans</i>, <i>bonificatus</i>, <i>bonificare</i>. This became the basis for proving that the divine principles are distinct yet equivalent in God (each principle has the same underlying threefold structure, yet retains its own unique correlatives). This supports the combinatorial operation of the <i>Art</i> (i.e., this means that in God goodness <i>is</i> greatness and greatness <i>is</i> goodness, goodness <i>is</i> eternity and eternity <i>is</i> goodness, etc.), the Lullian proof of the <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinity</a> (each divine principle has the three correlatives and together the principles comprise the Godhead, therefore the Godhead is threefold) and the <a href="/wiki/Incarnation" title="Incarnation">Incarnation</a> (the active and passive correlatives are equivalent to matter and form, and the trinitarian unfolding of being occurs on all levels of reality).<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_works">Other works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Other works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Influence_of_Islam_and_early_works">Influence of Islam and early works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Influence of Islam and early works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>It has been pointed out that the <i>Art</i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>s combinatorial mechanics bear a resemblance to <a href="/wiki/Zairja" title="Zairja">zairja</a>, a device used by medieval Arab astrologers.<sup id="cite_ref-link_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-link-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The Art's reliance on divine attributes also has a certain similarity to the contemplation of the <a href="/wiki/Names_of_God_in_Islam" title="Names of God in Islam">ninety-nine Names of God</a> in the Muslim tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Llull's familiarity with the Islamic intellectual tradition is evidenced by the fact that his first work (1271-2) was a compendium of <a href="/wiki/Al-Ghazali" title="Al-Ghazali">Al-Ghazali</a>'s logic.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Dialogues">Dialogues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Dialogues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>From early in his career Llull composed dialogues to enact the procedure of the <i>Art</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> This is linked to the missionary aspect of the <i>Art</i>. Llull conceived it as an instrument to convert all peoples of the world to Christianity and experimented with more popular genres to make it easier to understand. His earliest and most well known dialogue is the <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_the_Gentile_and_the_Three_Wise_Men" title="Book of the Gentile and the Three Wise Men">Book of the Gentile and the Three Wise Men</a></i>, written in Catalan the 1270s and later translated into Latin. It is framed as a meeting of three wise men (a Muslim, a Jew, and a Christian) and a Gentile in the woods. They learn about the Lullian method when they encounter a set of trees with leaves inscribed with Lullian principles. Lady Intelligence appears and informs them of the properties of the trees and the rules for implementing the leaves. The wise men use the trees to prove their respective Articles of Faith to the Gentile (although some of the Islamic tenets cannot be proved with the Lullian procedure) and in the end the Gentile is converted to Christianity. Llull also composed many other dialogues. Later in his career when he became concerned with heretical activity in the Arts Faculty of the University of Paris, he wrote "disputations" with philosophers as interlocutors.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> He also created a character for himself and he stars in many of these dialogues as the Christian wise man (for instance: <i>Liber de quaestione valde alta et profunda</i>, composed in 1311).
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tree_diagrams">Tree diagrams</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Tree diagrams">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull structured many of his works around trees. In some, like the <i>Book of the Gentile and the Three Wise Men</i>, the "leaves" of the trees stand for the combinatorial elements (principles) of the <i>Art</i>. In other works a series of trees shows how the <i>Art</i> generates all ("encyclopedic") knowledge. The <i><a href="/wiki/Tree_of_Science_(Ramon_Llull)" title="Tree of Science (Ramon Llull)">Tree of Science</a></i> (1295-6) comprises sixteen trees ranging from earthly and moral to divine and pedagogical.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Each tree is divided into seven parts (roots, trunk, branches, twigs, leaves, flowers, fruits). The roots always consist of the Lullian divine principles and from there the tree grows into the differentiated aspects of its respective category of reality.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Novels">Novels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Novels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramon_Llull_(Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg/220px-Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="372" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg/330px-Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg/440px-Ramon_Llull_%28Santuari_de_Nostra_Senyora_de_Cura%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="992" data-file-height="1676" /></a><figcaption>Ceramic ceiling light in the Sanctuary of Cura, <a href="/wiki/Puig_de_Randa" title="Puig de Randa">Puig de Randa</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Llull also wrote narrative prose drawing on the literary traditions of his time (<a href="/wiki/Epic_poetry" title="Epic poetry">epic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chivalric_romance" title="Chivalric romance">romance</a>) to express the <i>Art</i>. These works were intended to communicate the potentially complex operations of the <i>Art</i> to a lay audience. <i><a href="/wiki/Blanquerna" title="Blanquerna">Blanquerna</a></i> (c.1276-83) is his most well known novel. <i>Felix</i> (1287-9) is also notable, although it was not widely circulated during his lifetime and was only available in Catalan. It is formulated as a sort of <i><a href="/wiki/Bildungsroman" title="Bildungsroman">Bildungsroman</a></i> in which Felix, the main character, begins on a journey at the instigation of his father who has written the "Book of Wonders". The book is divided into ten chapters (echoing the encyclopedic range of the Tree of Science) as Felix gains knowledge: God, angels, heavens, elements, plans, minerals, animals, man, Paradise, and Hell. It turns out to be a <a href="/wiki/Metafiction" title="Metafiction">metafiction</a>, as Felix's journey ends at a monastery where he relates the "Book of Wonders" now embellished and fused with the account of his own adventures.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Reception">Reception</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Reception">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Lullism" title="Lullism">Lullism</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval">Medieval</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Medieval">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Academic_theology">Academic theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Academic theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>According to Llull's autobiographical <i>Vita</i>, his <i>Art</i> was not received well at the University of Paris when he first presented it there in the 1280s. This experience supposedly is what led him to revise the <i>Art</i> (creating the tertiary version). Llull's <i>Art</i> was never adopted by mainstream academia of the thirteenth and early-fourteenth centuries, but it did accrue quite a bit of interest. A significant number of Lullian manuscripts were collected by the <a href="/wiki/Carthusians" title="Carthusians">Carthusian</a> monks of Paris at Vauvert and by several theologians who donated their manuscripts to the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Sorbonne" title="College of Sorbonne">Sorbonne</a> Library. One disciple, Thomas Le Myésier, went so far as to create elaborate compilations of Llull's works, including a manuscript dedicated to the <a href="/wiki/Joan_II,_Countess_of_Burgundy" title="Joan II, Countess of Burgundy">queen of France</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramon_Llull,_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier,_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg/220px-Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg/330px-Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg/440px-Ramon_Llull%2C_with_his_disciple_Thomas_le_Myesier%2C_presenting_three_anthologies_to_the_Queen_of_France_and_Navarre.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1004" data-file-height="1255" /></a><figcaption>Ramon Llull, with his disciple Thomas Le Myésier, presenting three anthologies to the queen</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition">Opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In the 1360s the inquisitor <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Eymerich" title="Nicholas Eymerich">Nicholas Eymerich</a> condemned Lullism in Aragon. He obtained a <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> in 1376 to prohibit Lullian teaching, although it proved ineffective. In Paris <a href="/wiki/Jean_Gerson" title="Jean Gerson">Jean Gerson</a> also issued a series of polemical writings against Lullism. There was an official document issued to prohibit the Lullian <i>Art</i> from being taught in the Faculty of Theology.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_modern">Early modern</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Early modern">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Academic_theology_2">Academic theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Academic theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull's most significant early modern proponent was <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_of_Cusa" title="Nicholas of Cusa">Nicholas of Cusa</a>. He collected many works by Llull and adapted many aspects of Lullian thought for his own mystical theology.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> There was also growing interest in Lullism in Catalonia, Italy, and France. <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Lef%C3%A8vre_d%27%C3%89taples" title="Jacques Lefèvre d&#39;Étaples">Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples</a> published eight of Llull's books in 1499, 1505, and 1516. Lefèvre was therefore responsible for the first significant circulation of Llull's work in print outside of Catalonia.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> It is thought that the influence of Lullian works in Renaissance Italy (coinciding with the rise of <a href="/wiki/Platonism_in_the_Renaissance" title="Platonism in the Renaissance">neoplatonism</a>) contributed to a development in metaphysics, from a static Artistotelian notion of being to reality as a dynamic process.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In Northern and Central Europe Lullism was adopted by Lutherans and Calvinists interested in promoting programs of theological humanism. <a href="/wiki/Gottfried_Wilhelm_Leibniz" title="Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz">Gottfried Leibniz</a> was exposed to these currents during his years in Mainz, and Llull's <i>Art</i> clearly informed his <i><a href="/wiki/De_Arte_Combinatoria" title="De Arte Combinatoria">De Arte Combinatoria</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Pseudo-Llull_and_alchemy">Pseudo-Llull and alchemy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Pseudo-Llull and alchemy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>There is a significant body of alchemical treatises falsely attributed to Llull. The two fundamental works of the corpus are the <i>Testamentum</i> and the <i>Liber de secretis naturae seu de quinta essentia</i> which both date to the fourteenth century.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Occultists such as <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Cornelius_Agrippa" title="Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa">Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giordano_Bruno" title="Giordano Bruno">Giordano Bruno</a> were inspired by these works.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Despite Llull's growing identification with alchemy and Neoplatonic mysticism, others (such as <a href="/wiki/Giulio_Pace" title="Giulio Pace">Giulio Pace</a> and <a href="/wiki/Johann_Heinrich_Alsted" title="Johann Heinrich Alsted">Johann Heinrich Alsted</a>) were still interested in the Lullian <i>Art</i> as a universal logic, even in the seventeenth century when <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Descartes" title="René Descartes">Descartes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Petrus_Ramus" title="Petrus Ramus">Ramus</a> proposed competing systems.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Iberian_Revival_and_beatification">Iberian Revival and beatification</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Iberian Revival and beatification">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Meanwhile, in Spain, the Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Jim%C3%A9nez_de_Cisneros" title="Francisco Jiménez de Cisneros">Francisco Jiménez de Cisneros</a>, Archbishop of Toledo, had taken up Lullism for his project of reform. Cisneros mobilized various intellectuals and editors, founding chairs at universities and publishing Llull's works.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Founded in 1633, the Pontifical College of La Sapiencia on Majorca became the epicenter for teaching Lullism. The Franciscans from La Sapiencia were the ones to seek Llull's canonization at Rome in the seventeenth century. These efforts were renewed in the eighteenth century, but never succeeded.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Llull was <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> in 1847 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a>. His <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> was assigned to 30 June and is celebrated by the <a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_St._Francis" class="mw-redirect" title="Third Order of St. Francis">Third Order of St. Francis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-habig_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-habig-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Twentieth_and_twenty-first_centuries">Twentieth and twenty-first centuries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Twentieth and twenty-first centuries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Scholarship">Scholarship</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Scholarship">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull is now recognized by scholars as significant in both the history of Catalan literature as well as intellectual history. From 1906 to 1950 the Comissió Editora Lul·liana led a project to edit Llull's works written in Catalan. This series was called the Obres de Ramon Llull (ORL). In 1957 the Raimundus-Lullus-Institut was founded in Freiburg, Germany to begin the work of editing Llull's Latin works. This series is called the Raimundi Lulli Opera Latina (ROL) and is still ongoing.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In 1990 the work on the Catalan texts was restarted with the Nova Edició de les Obres de Ramon Llull (NEORL).<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In the world of English-language scholarship the work of <a href="/wiki/Frances_Yates" title="Frances Yates">Frances Yates</a> on memory systems (<i><a href="/wiki/The_Art_of_Memory" title="The Art of Memory">The Art of Memory</a></i>, published 1966) brought new interest to Ramon Llull as a figure in the history of cognitive systems.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Art_and_fiction">Art and fiction</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Art and fiction">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull has appeared in the art and literature of the last century, especially in the genres of <a href="/wiki/Surrealism" title="Surrealism">surrealism</a>, philosophical fantasy, and metafiction. <a href="/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD" title="Salvador Dalí">Salvador Dalí</a>'s alchemical thought was influenced by Ramon Llull and Dalí incorporated the diagrams from the Lullian <i>Art</i> into his work called <i>Alchimie des Philosophes</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> In 1937 <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Luis_Borges" title="Jorge Luis Borges">Jorge Luis Borges</a> wrote a snippet called "Ramon Llull' s Thinking Machine" proposing the Lullian <i>Art</i> as a device to produce poetry.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
Other notable references to Ramon Llull are: <a href="/wiki/Aldous_Huxley" title="Aldous Huxley">Aldous Huxley's</a> short story "The Death of Lully", a fictionalized account aftermath of Llull's stoning in Tunis, set aboard the Genoese ship that returned him to Mallorca.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Paul_Auster" title="Paul Auster">Paul Auster</a> refers to Llull (as Raymond Lull) in his memoir <i><a href="/wiki/The_Invention_of_Solitude" title="The Invention of Solitude">The Invention of Solitude</a></i> in the second part, <i>The Book of Memory</i>. Llull is also a major character in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Box_of_Delights" title="The Box of Delights">The Box of Delights</a></i>, a children's novel by poet <a href="/wiki/John_Masefield" title="John Masefield">John Masefield</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_recognition">Other recognition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Other recognition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Llull's <i>Art</i> is sometimes recognized as a precursor to <a href="/wiki/Computer_science" title="Computer science">computer science</a> and computation theory.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
With the discovery in 2001 of his lost manuscripts, <i>Ars notandi</i>, <i>Ars eleccionis</i>, and <i>Alia ars eleccionis</i>, Llull is also given credit for creating an <a href="/wiki/Electoral_system" title="Electoral system">electoral system</a> now known as the <a href="/wiki/Borda_count" title="Borda count">Borda count</a> and <a href="/wiki/Condorcet_criterion" class="mw-redirect" title="Condorcet criterion">Condorcet criterion</a>, which <a href="/wiki/Jean-Charles_de_Borda" title="Jean-Charles de Borda">Jean-Charles de Borda</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marquis_de_Condorcet" title="Marquis de Condorcet">Nicolas de Condorcet</a> independently proposed centuries later.<sup id="cite_ref-Hagele_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hagele-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Translations">Translations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramon_Llull&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Translations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>Ramon Llull's New Rhetoric</i>, text and translation of Llull's 'Rethorica Nova', edited and translated by Mark D. Johnston, Davis, California: Hermagoras Press, 1994</li>
<li><i>Selected Works of Ramon Llull (1232‑1316)</i>, edited and translated by Anthony Bonner, Princeton, N.J.: Princeton University Press 1985, two volumes XXXI + 1330 pp. (Contents: vol. 1: <i>The Book of the Gentile and the Three Wise Men</i>, pp.&#160;93–305; <i>Ars Demonstrativa</i>, pp.&#160;317–567; <i>Ars Brevis</i>, pp.&#160;579–646; vol. 2: <i>Felix: or the Book of Wonders</i>, pp.&#160;659–1107; <i>Principles of Medicine</i> pp.&#160;1119–1215; <i>Flowers of Love and Flowers of Intelligence</i>, pp.&#160;1223–1256)</li>
<li><i>Doctor Illuminatus: A Ramon Llull Reader</i>, edited and translated by Anthony Bonner, with a new translation of <i>The Book of the Lover and the Beloved</i> by Eve Bonner, Princeton, N.J.: Princeton University Press 1994</li></ul>
